    Calculation of $q^3 = 1.5$.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    $1.5 = 3/2$.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{3/2}$.

    Could there be a case where the GP terms are $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ but the indices don't align with blocks in a way that sums are $2n$?
    No, sums $S_{3n}$ are fixed.
    The partial sums are fixed.
    $S_3=2, S_6=6, S_9=12$.
    So $a_1+a_2+a_3=2$, $a_4+a_5+a_6=4$, $a_7+a_8+a_9=6$.
    These are hard constraints.
    My analysis relied on these hard constraints.
    So it should be correct.

    Let's quickly check $k=1$ again.
    GP $a_1, \dots, a_9$.
    $a_1=x$.
    $a_1+a_2+a_3 = x(1+q+q^2) = 2$.
    $a_4+a_5+a_6 = xq^3(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $a_7+a_8+a_9 = xq^6(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
    From 1st and 2nd: $q^3 = 4/2 = 2$.
    From 2nd and 3rd: $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    Contradiction.
    So $k=1$ impossible.

    Check $k=2$.
    GP $a_2, \dots, a_{10}$.
    $a_2=x$.
    $a_1+a_2+a_3 = a_1 + x + xq = 2$. (1)
    $a_4+a_5+a_6 = xq^2+xq^3+xq^4 = xq^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$. (2)
    $a_7+a_8+a_9 = xq^5+xq^6+xq^7 = xq^5(1+q+q^2) = 6$. (3)
    $a_{10}+a_{11}+a_{12} = xq^8+a_{11}+a_{12} = 8$. (4)
    From (2) and (3): $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    Then $x$ is determined by (2).
    $x = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    Then (1) determines $a_1$.
    $a_1 = 2 - x(1+q)$.
    Since $q \approx 1.14$, $x \approx 0.88$.
    $a_1 \approx 2 - 0.88(2.14) \approx 2 - 1.88 = 0.12 > 0$.
    (4) determines $a_{11}+a_{12}$.
    $a_{11}+a_{12} = 8 - xq^8 \approx 8 - 0.88(3.6) \approx 8 - 3.2 = 4.8$.
    Real numbers exist.
    So valid.

    Check $k=3$.
    GP $a_3, \dots, a_{11}$.
    $a_3=x$.
    $a_1+a_2+a_3 = a_1+a_2+x = 2$. (1)
    $a_4+a_5+a_6 = xq+xq^2+xq^3 = xq(1+q+q^2) = 4$. (2)
    $a_7+a_8+a_9 = xq^4+xq^5+xq^6 = xq^4(1+q+q^2) = 6$. (3)
    $a_{10}+a_{11}+a_{12} = xq^7+xq^8+a_{12} = 8$. (4)
    From (2) and (3): $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    $x = \frac{4}{q(1+q+q^2)}$.
    $a_1+a_2 = 2-x$.
    Can choose $a_1, a_2$.
    $a_{12} = 8 - xq^7 - xq^8$.
    Real.
    Valid.

    Check $k=4$.
    GP $a_4, \dots, a_{12}$.
    $a_4=x$.
    $a_1+a_2+a_3 = 2$. (No $x$).
    $a_4+a_5+a_6 = x(1+q+q^2) = 4$. (1)
    $a_7+a_8+a_9 = xq^3(1+q+q^2) = 6$. (2)
    $a_{10}+a_{11}+a_{12} = xq^6(1+q+q^2) = 8$. (3)
    From (1), (2): $q^3 = 6/4 = 1.5$.
    From (2), (3): $q^3 = 8/6 = 1.33$.
    Contradiction.
    So $k=4$ impossible.
    Wait, why did I think $k=4$ might work earlier?
    I said "Block 2, 3, 4 full".
    Yes, $a_4..a_6$ (Block 2), $a_7..a_9$ (Block 3), $a_{10}..a_{12}$ (Block 4).
    All 3 blocks fully in GP.
    Sums 4, 6, 8.
    Ratios 1.5, 1.33.
    Contradiction.
    So $k=4$ is impossible.
    My previous check for $k=4$ said "3 full blocks... impossible".
    So correct.

    So only $k=2, 3$ work.
    Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    Is there any case with $k$ such that we have 2 full blocks but not consecutive?
    We said no.
    Is there any case where we have 1 full block?
    We said no.
    Is there any case where we have 0 full blocks?
    No, length 9 > 3.

    Wait, what if the GP ratio $q$ is such that terms cancel out?
    e.g. $1+q+q^2 = 0$? No real $q$.
    What if $1+q+q^2$ is very small?
    Then $x$ must be large.
    But $x$ is just a number.
    Doesn't restrict $q$ other than existence.
    But consistency between blocks restricts $q$.

    One edge case: $n$ in $S_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    $n \in \mathbb{N}^* = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$.
    So sums are defined for $3, 6, 9, \dots$.
    Correct.

    Final check:
    Question asks for "q的最大值" (maximum value of q).
    Value is $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    Can be written $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Or $\frac{\sqrt[3]{6}}{2}$.

    Let's check if there is any interpretation where $a_n$ are not necessarily real?
    "实数q" - q is real.
    "数列{a_n}" - usually implies real sequence unless specified complex.
    Given "实数q", likely context is real numbers.
    If complex allowed, $1+q+q^2=0$ gives $q = e^{i \pi/3}$.
    But "maximum value" implies ordering, so real.

    Result $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$ seems robust.

    Let's convert to a nice form.
    $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Decimal 1.1447...

    Is there any integer constraint? No.
    Is it possible the answer is an integer? No.
    Is it possible the answer is rational? No.

    Wait, could $q$ be $3/2$?
    $q^3 = 1.5 \implies q = 1.14$.
    If $q=1.5$, $q^3 = 3.375$.
    Requires ratio 3.375.
    $3.375 = 27/8$.
    Need $\frac{n+1}{n} = 27/8 \implies 8n+8 = 27n \implies 19n = 8 \implies n=8/19$. Not integer.
    So no.

    What if the GP length was different?
    Problem says 9 terms.
    If it was 6 terms, we could have $k=1$ with blocks 1, 2 full?
    Length 6. Window $[1, 6]$.
    Blocks 1, 2 full.
    $q^3 = 4/2 = 2$.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{2}$.
    But length is 9.
    So $k=1$ forces 3 blocks.

    So the constraint of length 9 is crucial.
    And it eliminates the $n=1$ case (ratio 2).
    Leaving $n=2$ (ratio 1.5).

    Double check the "3 blocks" logic for $k=1$.
    Indices $1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9$.
    Block 1: $1, 2, 3$. All in window.
    Block 2: $4, 5, 6$. All in window.
    Block 3: $7, 8, 9$. All in window.
    Yes.
    Sums must satisfy geometric progression with ratio $q^3$.
    $B_1 = 2$.
    $B_2 = 4$.
    $B_3 = 6$.
    $B_2/B_1 = 2$.
    $B_3/B_2 = 1.5$.
    $2 \ne 1.5$.
    Contradiction.
    So $k=1$ impossible.
    Correct.

    What about $k=0$?
    Indices $0, 1, \dots$.
    But sequence starts at $a_1$? "N^*" usually $1, 2, \dots$.
    Also $S_{3n}$ defined for $n \in \mathbb{N}^*$.
    Implies $a_1$ exists.
    So $k \ge 1$.

    So max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    The value is $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
